Myles Garrett on three-sack game: It was a weight off my shoulders to get back to who I am

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Myles Garrett was Myles Garrett on Sunday.

Now healthy, Garrett made three sacks and had six pressures on 27 pass rushes in the Rams’ 20-13 win over Washington, according to NFL Next Gen Stats.

“Man, it was a weight off my shoulders, just getting back to who I am,” Garrett said, via Sarah Barshop of ESPN. “I’m nowhere near where I want to be as far as my performance and things I know I can do and how I can move, but this is a confidence booster for sure.”

Garrett tried to play through a knee injury in Week 1 and had no stats in 40 snaps. He underwent arthroscopic surgery the following week and had not played again until Sunday.

Garrett called his Week 1 performance “the worst version of myself that I put out there.”

Sunday marked the ninth time Garrett has had three sacks, and the reigning Defensive Player of the Year now has 128.5 sacks in his NFL career.

The Rams traded what they traded for Garrett for him to be the closer they haven’t had since Aaron Donald initially retired in the 2024 offseason. He did just that in his second game.

“I am that closer, so we don’t need to look any further,” Garrett said.

It was the first game Garrett and Donald played together. They were on the field at the same time for 23 plays and combined for four sacks.

China wholesale inflation hits highest in more than four years as consumer measure slows

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Producer prices in China grew at the fastest rate since June 2022, accelerating for the third month in a row.

The PPI for October came in at 5.7%, compared to the 5.3% expected by economists polled by Reuters. The increase was driven by a sharp rise in prices in the mining and raw materials industries.

The increase was driven by very strong external demand, Zhang Zhiwei, president and chief economist at Pinpoint Asset Management, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Consumer inflation in China slowed to 0.7% in October, its first deceleration after two straight months of increases.

The figure missed expectations of a 0.8% rise from economists polled by Reuters, and was lower than the 0.9% seen in September.

Core inflation, which strips out food and energy prices, came in at 0.8%, down from the 1% seen in the month before.

The rise in consumer prices were still held up by prices of gasoline, which was the largest contributor to the increase in prices, but this was offset by a sharper than expected fall in food prices, according to Dong Lijuan, chief statistician of the urban division at China's National Bureau of Statistics.

The Chinese economy has shown some signs of expansion recently. It grew faster than expected in the third quarter, and September saw a sharp beat in retail sales.

The mainland Chinese CSI 300 index gained 0.56% after the data release.

Pinpoint's Zhang said that domestic demand has been rather weak due to the property market, and this is a key reason why inflation is not picking up in China"There's no clear sign of stabilization [in that sector] yet," he said, adding this was weighing on the household consumer confidence and local government revenue.

"So the the overall economic momentum has been weak and hasn't really seen [a] clear sign of recovery," Zhang explained.

How to use your laptop as a second monitor

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Converting the device is not as easy as simply plugging in an HDMI cord, but here's what does work.

Whether you're gaming, working or simply browsing the web, adding a second monitor is one of the best ways to upgrade your workflow. Mirroring the display, which shows the same desktop on both screens, can be handy for presentations, while extending the display gives you one wider desktop. Extending the desktop into additional screen space means more visible tabs, easier multitasking and increased productivity. But making this change does not necessarily require buying a fancy setup. If you have a spare laptop lying around at home, you can make a few small tweaks to use dual monitors without going all-in on new hardware.

Converting your laptop into a second screen is fairly straightforward but not necessarily as simple as plugging in an HDMI cable. First you need to check your computer's compatibility, consider wired versus wireless options and tweak some settings on your system to smoothly extend or mirror the display. So how, exactly, do you turn your laptop into a second monitor? Here are the steps for Windows, Mac and Linux, as well as wired options via HDMI connections.

If both your primary computer and the spare laptop you want to use as a second monitor are running Windows 10 or 11, extending or mirroring them is quite simple due to the operating systems' built-in wireless casting tools. These rely on Miracast, a functionality that enables connections between devices via Wi-Fi and requires only a quick workflow to set up.

To activate it, go to your spare laptop, open Settings, then System, then Projecting to this PC. Click Optional features, then View features, then select Wireless Display to add it. Once it installs, return to the same page and set it to Available everywhere, and open the Wireless Display app.

Now, you're ready to mirror or extend your display. On your main computer, press the Windows key + K, select the spare laptop from the list of available displays, enter the PIN and allow keyboard and mouse input. You can then choose to duplicate your screen or extend it, and you can switch anytime with Windows key + P. Keep in mind, this method of connecting your devices does require Wi-Fi on both machines, and you may experience some lag. Therefore, while it's great for presentations or organizing large numbers of tabs, it may not be the best choice for gaming.

If both of your devices are Macs, you can use AirPlay to connect the two, but only if the MacBook is from 2018 or later. Once you've confirmed your device is compatible, go to the MacBook you want to use as the second screen, open System Settings, select General, then AirDrop & Continuity. Turn on AirPlay Receiver and then under Allow AirPlay for, select Current User. Make sure both MacBooks are signed into the same Apple ID to ensure a smooth connection. On your primary device, open System Settings, then Displays and click the plus sign from the dropdown menu to select your secondary device from the list. Decide if you want mirroring or extended display, which can be selected under settings. Like Windows' Miracast, Apple's AirPlay works over Wi-Fi and can thus have a slight delay. For a wired option with less lag, see the HDMI section below.

If connecting between two different operating systems, whether between a Windows PC and a MacBook or anything involving Linux, you will need software like Deskreen, a free, open-source tool that runs on Wi-Fi. To connect, make sure both computers are on the same Wi-Fi network, and download Deskreen on the main device. Deskreen will offer a QR code and URL. Open this on the spare laptop's browser to connect the devices. Deskreen mirrors the display by default. To extend to the spare laptop, you will need a cheap virtual display adaptor, or a "dummy plug," in your main computer's HDMI port. Like the other systems, any software that connects devices over Wi-Fi will have some lag, so consider the wired option below for gaming or other activities that rely on smoother connections.

Most laptop HDMI ports are video-out only, so plugging two devices together with a cord will not directly allow you to extend or mirror displays. To work around this, get an HDMI-to-USB video capture card, which can be plugged into both laptops. Plug your main device's HDMI cable into the card, then plug the card's USB end into the spare laptop. The capture card will function as a webcam, so you can view the feed in your laptop's camera app or a free video app like OBS Studio, which runs on Windows, Mac and Linux.

If you are gaming and want to play a console without a TV, you can connect a PlayStation or a Nintendo Switch to the laptop in the same way. Again, there may be a small amount of lag but usually less than Wi-Fi-based connections, and it's unlikely to trip you up unless you are a competitive gamer. If none of these uses appeal to you, you can always use your old laptop to make a smart home hub.

Liverpool vs. Man United: Michael Carrick injury boost as "spasm" timeline revealed before Anfield Premier League game

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

By Lewis Nolan | 09 Nov 2026 01:17

Manchester United boss Michael Carrick has confirmed that Harry Maguire should have enough time to recover for their clash with Liverpool after a back spasm.

The Red Devils will enter the international break in 18th place following their 4-4 draw with Aston Villa on Sunday, with the club taking just eight points from their first 10 games of the Premier League season.

To make matters worse, centre-back Maguire was replaced at half time at Old Trafford, and fears arose that he could miss the team's match against Liverpool on November 22.

However, Carrick eased those concerns and suggested that the international break could give him enough time to recover, telling MUTV: "Harry had a bad back. A really bad back spasm that he had with maybe 10, 15 minutes to go in the first half.

"I don't know how bad it is but, going into the internationals, there should be plenty of time for him to recover."

Ayden Heaven came on to replace Maguire, though Lisandro Martinez will also be available for United's visit to Anfield.

The game at Anfield could be defining for United for a number of reasons, but none more so than the fact a damaging defeat could be Carrick's last ever match in charge as boss.

There is a chance that he could be dismissed during the international break, though the fixture against Liverpool will still be vital considering a loss would be a worrying way for a new head coach to start their reign.

More concerning is that failure to win could leave the Red Devils last in the table after 11 matchweeks, and that would likely condemn them to a relegation battle for the remainder of the campaign.

A meeting with Liverpool will always be a significant event, but there is far more at stake than just another three points, so having as many players available for that fixture as possible will be important.

Liverpool lead the division in terms of points taken on the road this season (13), but they currently rank 18th for points taken at home (5).

The Reds have already faced Manchester City and Arsenal at Anfield, though it was demoralising for fans to see the team sink to 3-0 and 1-0 defeats in those contests.

A commanding victory against United would be Andoni Iraola's best result of the season, and it would fuel optimism in the fanbase that he could lead the Merseysiders to success.

Nvidia's board is investigating CEO Jensen Huang's ties to a suspected Chinese spy

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Huang apologized to staff for being unfaithful to his wife, and the board said it has no reason to believe company secrets were compromised

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang (I-Hwa Cheng/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Nvidia $NVDA's board of directors opened an independent investigation on Sunday after reports surfaced that CEO Jensen Huang was targeted by a foreign intelligence operation, the company said. A panel made up of independent board members, with support from outside legal advisers, will supervise the inquiry.

Huang apologized to staff in a message for his infidelity toward his spouse. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," he wrote. Huang said he supports the investigation and will cooperate.

Jensen Huang disclosed in a note to employees that Alice Huang was the woman involved in his affair. Alice Huang is a business executive believed to be an operative for Chinese intelligence, according to The Wall Street Journal. She is no relation to the Nvidia CEO.

The board said it has seen nothing suggesting that U.S. security interests or Nvidia's proprietary information were put at risk, and that its review will draw on whatever information it can gather. The board said it has confidence in Jensen Huang and his leadership team and will provide updates as appropriate.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption," a Nvidia spokesperson said in a statement to The Journal.

U.S. officials concluded that Alice Huang, then a top executive at Megaspeed International, passed information to the Chinese government during her work with Nvidia on building data centers in Asia, according to The Journal. Megaspeed has said it is a "compliant and legitimate company." The Chinese Embassy in Washington rejected the spying allegations.

Jensen Huang said last month that Nvidia's business with Megaspeed was "normal and not unusual". He said Nvidia had verified that Megaspeed did not ship its AI processors to China in violation of the law. Nvidia spokesman John Rizzo said at the time that Jensen Huang's interactions with Alice Huang were "not unusual or unexpected."

Three House Republican committee chairs wrote to Jensen Huang on Wednesday requesting that he appear for a private session with lawmakers on Nov. 18. Sen. Chris Coons (D., Del.) vowed to look into the issue.

Nvidia, which has a market capitalization of $4.5 trillion, is scheduled to release its results for the third quarter on Nov. 17.

Nick Jonas Details What He "Resents" About His Diabetes

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Nick Jonas shed light on his decades-long journey with type 1 diabetes, revealing what he’s “grateful” for yet “resents” about the disease—which he was diagnosed with in 2005—in an open letter.

Nick Jonas is sharing insight into his health.

More than two decades after he was diagnosed with type 1 diabetes, the Jonas Brothers member detailed his complicated relationship with the disease in an open letter to his “old friend.”

“How long has it been? 21 years. 21 years, and you haven’t changed a bit,” Jonas wrote in the letter, which is a part of Dexcom’s A Letter to Diabetes initiative. “It’s funny how often we talk. Every few minutes, honestly. Staring at my phone to see where my glucose is. Reaching for my pump to bolus because I’m about to eat something with 17 carbs.”

“Those are just a couple of the hundreds of little decisions I make every week,” he continued. “Most people never notice them. But they add up to a real relationship, because that’s what diabetes is. It’s an ongoing conversation between your body, your mind, and your devices.”

The “Burnin’ Up” singer noted that there are days it feels like he and his condition are “on the same team,” while on others it seems like his diabetes is on a “solo mission.”

As Jonas—dad to daughter Malti, 4, with wife Priyanka Chopra—put it, “In a lot of ways, that’s what family is like. You rely on each other, you push each other, and you know they’ll always be there.”

But when the “A Little Bit Longer” artist was first diagnosed with diabetes in November 2005 at age 13, its “unpredictability threw” him for a loop.

“I’d built my whole life around this unrealistic structure and belief that I was in control,” he wrote. “You took that apart over the years, bit by bit. Part of me is grateful I learned that lesson. Part of me resents you for it. Full stop.”

Jonas then admitted, “There are days I wonder what life would feel like without you.”

That said, the Camp Rock star emphasized that being diagnosed with diabetes brought him something unexpected: a strong “community.”

“I always struggled to feel like I belonged anywhere,” Jonas recalled. “At school, I felt misunderstood. In most circles, for that matter. Through my childhood and youth, that was the theme. It wasn’t until I started talking openly about life with type 1, and meeting other people who were living it too, that I finally felt like I belonged somewhere.”

And while the “Jealous” singer “never wanted” to been painted as a “victim,” he continues to be open about his “truth” to help others feel “a little less alone.”

“I share my story for the 13-year-old who just got diagnosed and is lying in a hospital bed, scared to death,” Jonas noted. “I want them to be able to look up and see someone out there living their dreams while managing all of this, every single day.”

To see what other stars had to say about their journeys with diabetes, keep reading…

Nick Jonas can hit up the waffle house with an appetite and the know-how to manage his glucose levels.

The Jonas Brothers member has become one of the most high-profile stars with type 1 diabetes, with his diagnosis at 13 years old coming right before the sibling trio hit it big on the Disney Channel and with their 2006 debut album It's About Time.

Years later, Nick co-founded the nonprofit Beyond Type 1. Per its website, the organization “aims to change what it means to live with chronic illness” by “bridging the gap from diagnosis to cure, empowering people to live well today and funding a better tomorrow.”

Since his diagnosis, brothers Kevin and Joe have had Nick’s back as he monitors his health while keeping up with their rock-and-roll schedule.

“They’re incredibly supportive, and really help normalize it,” Nick explained to TODAY.com in 2023. “They understand that if I was irritable for an hour, that it’s probably because my glucose is high. And they give me grace, or make sure that if I’m starting to go low that I’ve got the juice I need or whatever at my disposal quickly.”

While a majority of people with type 1 diabetes are diagnosed in childhood, a stunned Lance Bass was in his 40s.

The *NSYNC singer detailed his diagnosis in a 2024 Instagram video, revealing he was previously misdiagnosed with type 2 diabetes, when in reality, he had developed type 1.5 diabetes.

Initially, "I had a difficult time getting my glucose levels under control, even though I made adjustments to my diet, my medications and my workout routine,” Lance said. “Things just weren't adding up.”

He now wears a Dexcom Continuous Glucose Monitor attached to his body. It takes readings of blood glucose levels every few minutes and sends alerts to a phone or receiver if it's changing rapidly so the person can potentially eat something carb-heavy if it's falling too fast or give themselves extra insulin if it's rising.

Meanwhile, Lance and husband Michael Turchin's twins, Alexander and Violet, 3, have taken an interest in his diabetes management routines as they play doctor at home.

"I'll show them the little needle that sticks out, and they just think it's fascinating," he said about his blood glucose monitor. “They're not scared of it at all. I explain over and over why I do this and what diabetes is, and you know, I don't know how much of that they're understanding. But you know, eventually they will."

Este Haim, seen above rocking her CGM while also rocking out onstage during a Haim concert alongside sisters Danielle Haim and Alana Haim, revealed how living with T1D has impacted her love life.

“I had a guy break up with me because I said that there was a possibility that our kid would have diabetes," she told British GQ in June 2025. "He was like, ‘Then why are we here?’ And I was like, ‘What?!'“

But it wasn’t the first time Haim endured disappointing reactions to her chronic illness, which she’s been navigating since her diagnosis at 14 years old.

“I was basically a social pariah for a very long time,” she told Beyond Type 1 in 2019. “You know, I was just the kid with diabetes in school that passes out and no one knows why and the girl with diabetes that smelled like orange juice all the time.”

Thankfully, like Nick Jonas, Este’s siblings have consistently shown up for her as she navigates tour life. “I’m lucky that I have Danielle and Alana to be my support," Haim said, "and when I am having a bad day, a bad week, a bad month, they’re always the first people to be like, ‘Dude we got you. Whatever you need. We’re going to get through this. Let’s get you a healthy meal. Let’s not have pizza after the show tonight. Let’s go get you something good so that you don’t wake up and your blood sugar is 350.’”

Bret Michaels has detailed the thorns poking out of his rosy career.

In a June 2025 Instagram post, the Poison frontman explained how living with type 1 diabetes might impact his tour schedule after a health scare.

“Unfortunately, the highs and lows of living with diabetes since I've been six years old occasionally just kick my butt,” Michaels wrote. “Last night, I went on stage and even added a song or two to the set to prove to myself & the fans that I could do it. At the end, my blood sugar walking off the stage was a real-life 39.”

Per the CDC, 70 mg / dL is considered low blood sugar for people with T1D—and severe low blood sugar begins at 54 mg / dL.

“I vow in the near future, I'm going to take that time needed,” he told fans. “And please forgive me if I have to move some of the shows around until I get this under control.

That’s not to say, Michaels hasn’t taken plenty of precautions along his decades-spanning career.

"I wake up and the first thing I do is check my constant glucose monitor," Michaels told People in 2024. "I take an injection and then the next thing I do is I get up and—good, bad or indifferent, no matter how I'm feeling—I get into a workout routine, and it helps to level the blood sugar out.”

He added, "And then I jump into the fun and the business of the day, and just try to take every day like that."

John Legend and Chrissy Teigen's son Miles

John Legend and Chrissy Teigen are proving to be extraordinary people when it comes to their son Miles' type 1 diagnosis.

The Cravings cookbook author detailed their shock upon discovering his T1D status on, after Miles was hospitalized for an unrelated health issue. “I've learned since then that this is how so many young children end up being diagnosed with type-1," she wrote on Instagram in July 2024, "going to the hospital for something completely different."

Teigen—also mom to Lunda, Esti and Wren—later shared how this inspired her to become an advocate with advanced screening for T1D. “I was blown away by the fact that not only did I not know that screening was an option when I first had children—I have four kids by the way—I didn’t hear about early screening at any point during any of my pregnancies,” she said at the Women’s Health Lab panel in May 2025. “It would’ve made an incredible amount of difference.”

The Dancing With the Stars pro shared her Type 1 diabetes diagnosis in May 2024, and has since been open about the strength she's earned through her experience.

“It’s a hard diagnosis and it takes a lot of time and just thinking out of your life, but I think it’s made me stronger and more mature,” she told Woman’s World in a July 2025 interview. “I’m able to handle conflict and hard things in my life better, because I go through the management and just the hard things that come with type one diabetes.”

Plus, she added, “It brings me so much motivation every day, because I want to be an inspiration to others. I want to be an inspiration to young people or anyone with [any] type of diabetes, so if it really gives me that motivation every day that I’m doing it for them and not just for myself.”

Hoda Kotb got some breaking news about her daughter Hope in 2023.

Yet, the former Today anchor waited until after she left the morning show full-time to reveal her youngest child was diagnosed with type 1 diabetes.

“Hope is fine for most of the day. There are just moments where you have to watch her," she told former cohosts Craig Melvin and Savannah Guthrie when returning to Studio 1A in May 2025. "I was totaling it up—five minutes at breakfast, five minutes at lunch, five minutes at dinner, sometimes overnight. Add that up, that's a half-hour.”

“So, for 23 and a half hours,” Kotb added. “She's every other kid. So, I try to remember that."

Kate Moss' model daughter Lila Moss celebrated the debut of some diabetes-chic accessories made for a groundbreaking Barbie.

A continuous glucose monitor and insulin pump are signature features of Mattel’s first-ever doll with type 1 diabetes. As the company noted in a July 2025 statement, its collaboration with Breakthrough T1D was "designed with inclusion and empathy at the core."

To celebrate the release, Mattel honored two type 1 diabetes ambassadors, Peloton instructor Robin Arzón and Moss, presenting them with their own one-of-a-kind dolls.

Upon the Barbie’s reveal, Moss wrote on Instagram, "This has been such a special project to be a part of, I hope this can encourage anyone living with Type 1 to not be afraid of wearing their pump proudly and that it helps to raise awareness on why we have them and what they do for us!"

The Airbus A350 Has Become The New Flagship Of Some Airlines While Others Stick With The Boeing 777

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

InsectWorld & Markus Mainka | Shutterstock , Simple Flying

Air Canada is about to take its first Airbus widebody in nearly three decades. Emirates, meanwhile, added 65 more Boeing 777-9s last November to an order book that now stands at 270. Same industry, same decade, opposite flagships. According to Business Traveller, Air Canada's eight A350-1000s begin arriving in the second half of 2030, while Emirates says its first 777-9 should land in the second quarter of 2027.

The split is not about brand loyalty, and it is messier than the headline suggests. Qatar Airways flies the A350-1000 and has ordered the 777-9. Emirates, the world's biggest 777 operator, also flies A350s. Behind those choices are fleet age, delivery schedules, route networks, passenger capacity and, in one particularly important case, an engine. The timing of each airline's decision matters almost as much as the aircraft itself.

Qatar Airways was the A350-1000's launch operator, putting it into service in February 2018, as Simple Flying previously reported. Japan Airlines(JAL), Delta Air Lines, Air Canada, and Qantas have since built parts of their long-haul plans around the larger Airbus, and British Airways flies it too. Riyadh Air and Philippine Airlines have also ordered the type.

The Boeing side has a different starting point. Emirates operates the largest 777 fleet on Earth, about 140 aircraft, based on the 119 777-300ERs, 10 777-200LRs, and 11 freighters. United Airlines flies nearly 100 777s and no A350s at all. American Airlines is retrofitting its 777-300ERs, while Lufthansa is due to become the first airline to fly the 777-9 in 2027. The 777-9 first flew in January 2020 and remains uncertified.

Timing accounts for much of the difference. By June 2026, the A350-1000 had 367 orders and 114 deliveries. The 777-9 was still uncertified and targeting a first delivery roughly seven years late. Airlines with aging 777-300ERs could not wait for Boeing's schedule to settle. Others had enough life left in their fleets to take the risk.

JAL offers the cleanest example. Its A350-1000 entered service between Tokyo Haneda Airport (HND) and John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK) in January 2024, and daily Los Angeles flights followed on June 30, 2025, according to Japan.travel. Delta will add 20 A350-1000s from early 2027 on top of 41 A350-900s already flying. Delta says the new jet burns more than 20 percent less fuel per available seat mile than the aircraft it replaces, and it will debut a next-generation Delta One Suite with a flat bed more than three inches (7.6 cm) longer, giving over six and a half feet (two meters) to stretch out.

Air Canada's reasoning is the most revealing. The A350-1000 is a logical stand-in for the 777-300ER, with a 19 to 25 percent cut in fuel burn per passenger-nautical mile and a cabin altitude of about 6,000 feet (1,829 meters) instead of the 777 family's 8,000 feet (2,438 meters).

A firm delivery slot mattered more to Air Canada than a bigger airplane on paper. Even so, the airline is not leaving Boeing: it operates 19 777-300ERs and expects 14 787-10s, so the A350 is diversification, not defection. The most dramatic conversion belongs to Qantas, because it is retiring a beloved superjumbo years ahead of schedule.

The 777X is nearly ready for service, how will it fare against Airbus's A350?

According to Executive Traveller, Qantas will gradually phase out its 10 A380s from 2028, roughly four years earlier than a plan that pointed to fiscal 2032. Qantas took its first A380 in September 2008 and its last in December 2011, so the oldest are approaching two decades of service. Lufthansa and Etihad Airways look set to keep their superjumbos into the early 2030s, while Korean Air and Qatar Airways are likely to follow Qantas out. CEO Vanessa Hudson cited rising maintenance costs and high fuel prices tied to the Middle East conflict. The A350-1000 will take over the flagship routes from Sydney to Singapore, London, and Los Angeles.

The transition away from the A380 will not be without capacity challenges. According to Qantas, its A380s have 485 seats, compared with an indicative capacity of more than 250 seats for the planned A350-1000LR replacements. The timing also presents a challenge: as Hudson reportedly said the additional replacement aircraft would not arrive until after 2030. In the meantime, 787s are expected to cover A380 services to Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport (DFW) and Johannesburg.

Qantas expects its 12 Project Sunrise A350-1000ULRs to help offset the loss of A380 capacity. Some will operate Perth–London and Auckland–New York services, freeing up 787s for other routes. Designed for ultra-long-haul flights such as Sydney–London and Sydney–New York, the aircraft completed its maiden flight on June 2. Its additional 20,000-liters fuel tank supports flights of up to 22 hours, helping Qantas expand its ultra-long-haul network.

Consider two routes. Dubai–London, at roughly 2,900 nautical miles (5,370 km), favors the 777-9's approximately 16% seating advantage, as capacity helps reduce cost per passenger. Perth–London, at around 7,800 nautical miles (14,450 km), favors the A350-1000, with a range of 8,700 nautical miles (16,112 km), compared with 8,000 nautical miles (14,816 km) for the 777-9.

Airbus also notes that carbon-fiber composites account for 53% of the A350-1000's structural weight, helping reduce fuel consumption and maintenance requirements. The same industry, but very different economics. The A350 therefore has an advantage on longer routes, particularly where range and delivery timing matter. Yet some of the world's biggest airlines continue to favor Boeing.

For Emirates, the answer is largely about scale. Its order for 65 more 777-9s, worth US$38 billion at list prices, pushed its 777X total to 270, with first deliveries expected from the second quarter of 2027. The 777-9 seats 375 to 450 passengers against 350 to 410 on the A350-1000, and on dense trunk routes that gap is significant. Emirates chairman Sheikh Ahmed bin Saeed Al Maktoum has also said the airline wants manufacturers to build larger aircraft, and Emirates holds options to convert some orders to the 777-10 or 777-8. According to Executive Traveller, it plans to keep its A380s flying until 2040.

American Airlines took a middle road. Its first retrofitted 777-300ER, which began flying from New York to Buenos Aires on September 2, offers 144 premium seats, including 70 Flagship Suites, across a fleet of 20. Flagship First will no longer be sold on those aircraft for travel from November 19. United Airlines' picture is stranger. As of June 30, the airline still lists 45 A350-900s as firm commitments but assigns no delivery dates, amid a $175 million dispute with Rolls-Royce. United's position was considerably different in 2022. According to Reuters , the airline was then considering a triple-digit widebody order involving either 787s or A350s.

Kirby's comments reflected the scale of United's fleet-renewal ambitions, although the airline ultimately committed to 100 787s in December 2022. United has since built a 787 order book of 146 firm commitments, which gives it an easy way out. One name keeps turning up in the background: Rolls-Royce. The engine choice on the A350-1000 has become part of the fleet decision itself.

The list is dominated by Qatar Airways' and Cathay Pacific's flights to North America.

The A350-1000 has a single engine option, the Trent XWB-97, and Emirates president Tim Clark has called it defective, with engines that, by one account, stay on the wing for only about a quarter of the time Emirates needs between maintenance visits. Airbus said this month that a durability package aims to double time on the wing from 2028, though that improvement has not yet been demonstrated in airline service. Rolls-Royce has committed £1 billion across its Trent Engine family, with much of it aimed at the XWB-97, a sign of how seriously it takes the problem.

Delivery certainty may still outweigh the criticism. Emirates reportedly weighed an order for at least 30 A350-1000s, and Simple Flying previously explored why the logic works: the Boeing 777X remains unavailable, while the A350-900 already performs well in Dubai, and the two A350 variants share a type rating and systems architecture, so pilots move between them with minimal extra training. Emirates counts 13 A350-900s in service and 52 on order. Even the Boeing camp's flagship customer is quietly buying Airbus. The planemaker lists certification and first delivery of the Qantas-bound A350-1000ULR for 2027, so the long-range case is about to move from demonstration to service.

Neither aircraft has to win every route. For airlines, the choice comes down to range, capacity, costs and availability. In some cases, the smartest move is not choosing one over the other, but using both where they make the most sense.

Some airlines are buying both jets. Qatar Airways, Cathay Pacific, and British Airways already fly the A350-1000 and have 777-9s on order. Lufthansa has ordered both as well, and its first A350-1000 arrives in mid-November. Meanwhile, Singapore Airlines has ordered only the 777-9. The usual logic is straightforward: the larger 777 suits the busiest routes, while the A350 can cover missions where range and efficiency matter more. Airbus is also studying a stretched version, informally called the A350-2000, to fill the gap between the A350-1000 and the 777X, although a formal launch depends on how airlines assess the market once the 777X enters service.

Airbus will likely have well over 150 A350-1000s flying by the time Boeing delivers its first 777-9 in 2027. That gives the A350 a clear advantage: airlines can look at years of operating experience rather than projected performance figures. Airlines needing aircraft now have few practical alternatives to the A350-1000, and the choice gets harder for deliveries between 2027 and 2030. Air Canada's slots only begin in late 2030, showing how far ahead some large widebody campaigns are being planned.

Boeing still leads the order book, with more than 520 777-9s against 367 A350-1000s. Airbus, however, has already delivered 114 A350-1000s. The next few 777-9 deliveries will therefore be important beyond the headline numbers. If Lufthansa's first aircraft slips again, airlines with aging 777-300ERs will have another reason to look at Airbus. If Emirates turns its reported A350-1000 interest into a firm order, the decision becomes harder to describe as a simple Airbus-versus-Boeing split.

Zelda: Ocarina of Time and WoW: Forever Are the Good Kind of Remakes

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Zelda: Ocarina of Time and World of Warcraft: Forever were made for different reasons than most remakes, and you can tell

Why are there so many video game remakes these days? We think we know the answer. New ideas are hard to come by, and big games are harder, more expensive, and take longer to make than ever before. Even a new sequel in an already-popular franchise is a huge, risky undertaking. But game companies need reliable products to put on the shelves quicker than they can make new ones. Remakes answer that need, as well as keeping back catalogs relevant for an industry whose relentless technological advances swiftly render classics obsolete.

That's all accurate. But what if the game developers working on remakes had reasons of their own for revisiting the classics — reasons that weren't just learning their way around Unreal Engine 5, plugging a gap between new releases, or answering market forces? We got an emphatic answer this week, in the form of not one but two remarkable new versions of two of the greatest and most influential games ever made. Those two games, released on consecutive days, are The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time and World of Warcraft: Forever.

A few months ago, I said that we need to decide what we want from remakes. This is what we want.

Nintendo’s excellent Switch 2 remake recaptures the soul of a masterpiece

Blizzard's retro MMO is about to sail into uncharted waters, but right now, it's clearly something special

These games have some similarities in their approach to remaking a classic, and some differences. Both games make smart modernizations while working hard to retain the spirit of the original. Ocarina of Time is a faithful recreation of a masterpiece that takes few liberties with its content and design, but makes a forceful departure in its lavish and technically dazzling art style.

WoW: Forever preserves the original WoW's art beneath a subtly impressive new rendering layer, and is built out from the same content. But it makes substantial additions, gets its hands dirty under the hood of the design, and, with its promise of a fixed progression cap and "horizontal" expansion, even seeks to boldly recontextualize WoW — and MMOs in general, and maybe even live-service games — in a new form.

In both cases, the developers have talked in interviews about their desire to preserve and carry forward something intrinsic about the games' design that they think has been lost in modern games.

Nintendo's top Legend of Zelda producers talk the remake, the future, and more

In a Nintendo "Ask the Developer" interview, Zelda producer Eiji Aonuma spoke about his desire to educate a new generation of Zelda developers who worked on Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom and weren't intimately familiar with the intricate mechanical mesh of classic Zelda design.

"By learning the game structure and development style behind the original The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, and by actually rebuilding it as a full remake, it may trigger new insights that they can carry forward into future development," Aonuma said. "If we could achieve that, I thought it would be incredibly valuable. That’s what led us to the conclusion that now is the right time to revisit The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time once again."

It's incredible to think that Nintendo's primary motivation in mounting this expensive production might have been bolstering institutional knowledge. But that's what makes Nintendo Nintendo.

The grassroots reimagining of classic World of Warcraft seems huge, but the team wants to stay small

Meanwhile, at Bizzard, a desire to see and play a "Classic Plus" version of WoW bubbled up naturally among the developers at the same time as it did in the WoW Classic community. This makes sense, since the WoW Classic team was mostly staffed by enthusiasts of the original game. It was the small, enterprising Classic team that experimented with the idea during the Season of Discovery event and then pitched it to leadership.

Lead Forever designer Kris Zierhut, who admits to having "drifted away" from modern WoW, explained it to me as a kind of thought experiment, a chance to imagine a different path the game might have taken. "Imagine if that team from 2004 — you know, Jeff Kaplan, and Eric Dodds, and Tom Chilton, and Scott Mercer, Pat Nagle, all those people — they've given them two more years to develop the game and add more stuff to it and perfect it," he said. "What would they have done with the time?"

What Zierhut and Aonuma are articulating is not a desire to preserve these epochal game designs for posterity. It's a desire to get inside them and explore them, find out what they can teach us, and maybe point out some things we've lost to progress — like an epic adventure game that puts craft before sheer scale, or a live-service game that's about the journey and not the destination.

Both these remakes were made with sincerity, passion, and above all, purpose. You don't have to take my word for it, or the developers'; you can tell when you play them. That's why they're so exciting, and how they honor two classics while bringing them alive for a new generation of players. Don't be too quick to dismiss remakes as cynical cash-grabs and schedule fillers. They can be beautiful acts of creation in their own right.

This week on Point It Out, Patches assembled an all star roundtable of indie gaming creators to discuss the state of the industry, its future and what it takes to make successful games like Shovel Knight, Hyper Light Drifter, Mina and more. Plus, WoW Forever is officially coming out, and Arkane Lyon may have a buyer. All this and more in the bouchon that is Point It Out!

A legendary lawman becomes a cyborg in the Netflix series

Hut 8 Expands AI Campus Commitments While Funding Buybacks With Bitcoin Sales

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Hut 8 says it fully commercialised its Beacon Point AI campus with a second 352 MW lease, taking expected aggregate base-term contract value across its AI data-centre portfolio to about $26.6 billion. The company also sold Bitcoin to fund share repurchases, showing how mining-era assets are being redeployed.

Hut 8’s third-quarter update is less about how many coins it mined and more about how much power, construction capacity and long-term tenant demand it can assemble. That represents a significant shift for a company long associated with Bitcoin infrastructure.

In its November 4 results, Hut 8 said a second 352-megawatt IT lease at Beacon Point fully commercialised the campus. It placed expected aggregate base-term contract value across its AI data-centre portfolio at about $26.6 billion and reported 1,330 MW of utility capacity under construction across its major AI projects.

The numbers describe commitments over years, not cash that has already appeared in the bank. Hut 8’s timetable includes an initial River Bend data-hall delivery target in the second quarter of 2027, followed by Beacon Point Phase 1 in the third quarter of 2027 and Phase 2 in the second quarter of 2028.

Those future dates matter. A contracted tenant can improve the business case for a development, but execution still depends on power availability, construction, testing, financing and customer readiness. The risk profile is not identical to Bitcoin mining, where machines may be deployed in stages and production responds directly to network conditions.

Hut 8 also reported 1,300 MW of additional energy capacity entering exclusivity, bringing that category to 3,180 MW. Exclusivity is a development milestone; it should not be interpreted as an operating facility or a guaranteed utility connection. Investors need to track the movement from a pipeline into actual delivered infrastructure.

The company said it bought back $74.7 million of shares during the quarter, funded with proceeds from selling approximately 954 Bitcoin. It later repurchased another $175.3 million of shares, funded through the sale of roughly 2,148 additional Bitcoin. Subsequent to the quarter, the repurchase authorisation increased to $1 billion.

That is a revealing capital-allocation decision. Selling Bitcoin to repurchase shares reduces treasury exposure to the coin while potentially increasing each remaining shareholder’s proportional interest in the company. Whether that creates value depends on the repurchase price and long-term performance of the business.

Hut 8 also closed a $1.07 billion senior secured revolving credit facility. The existence of a facility adds flexibility, but readers should distinguish committed borrowing capacity from cash revenue or money actually drawn. NewsBTC has previously examined the financing pressure created by miners’ AI expansion, and Hut 8 offers a clear example of how those choices reach the balance sheet.

Hut 8 has a substantial development pipeline and named delivery targets. The crucial tests will be whether those targets are met, whether project-level financing stays on acceptable terms and how much profit flows through when sites become operational.

The update captures a broader change in listed Bitcoin infrastructure businesses. Their market stories increasingly revolve around electricity access and long-term AI customers rather than the number of Bitcoin retained. That can produce a different revenue profile, but it does not remove the need for disciplined spending or independently verifiable execution.

Why your USB device isn't showing up on your MacBook and how you can fix it

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Fix hardware and software issues with some simple solutions.

USB wouldn't be what it is today without Apple. The format predates the original iconic iMac — but Apple's gumdrop-shaped computer helped popularize it by ditching all the legacy connectors that had been haunting the industry. USB became so commonplace, in fact, that the push came back to bite the company, with European Union regulations forcing it to ditch Lightning ports on iPhones. The days of Apple-exclusive ports are effectively over, MagSafe being the major remaining holdout.

Modern USB Mac accessories tend to be well-behaved. On occasion, though, macOS can fail to detect them, which is understandably maddening. With USB being as widespread as it is, anything less than a seamless experience feels unacceptable.

The good news is that there are known hardware and software explanations for why this happens, like damaged cords or an accessory that requires more power. In many situations the fixes are remarkably simple, particularly on the macOS side. There are rare instances, however, where you may need to seek outside help.

The first thing to remember is that by default, macOS requires you to give permission for accessories to connect. This prevents malware from being injected. At the same time, if you accidentally reject a permission prompt, a legitimate accessory won't transmit data until you give the green light. Charging should still work in either direction.

Typically, you can force macOS to re-display the permission prompt by unplugging an accessory and plugging it back in again. To minimize future hassle, though, go to Apple menu > System Settings > Privacy & Security, then scroll down to the Accessories section. Here you can choose Ask for new accessories, Automatically allow when unlocked or Always allow. It's best to skip that third option unless you're always around your Mac or in a very secure environment.

If that doesn't change anything, you might want to verify that macOS sees your device in the first place. Hold down the Option key, then click on Apple menu > System Information. If you see the accessory listed under Hardware, you may need to check the manufacturer's online support info. If it's invisible, or if it's detected but you keep getting an error message when plugging in, there may be more we can help with.

A probable cause in that regard is power. Some accessories may require more than a Mac's USB port can supply, in which case you should get a related message, and there may be a separate power cable you need to plug in. If there's no separate power on your accessory, however, the USB port itself may be the issue. Try alternate ports on your Mac or connecting directly if you were using a hub or dock as an intermediary. If you keep getting power-related warnings, you may want to talk to Apple tech support — it could be that your Mac just isn't feeding power to USB.

Speaking of hubs and docks, don't forget that those often require their own power supplies. And even if they don't, things can go wrong with device chains, since of course they have to successfully transmit power or data across multiple hops. We recently had an experience with a Thunderbolt dock in which a wireless mouse receiver became unusable until it was plugged directly into a laptop — never mind that an SSD on the dock was doing just fine. It's always safer to skip the middleman if you have enough ports free.

Make sure that macOS is fully up-to-date by going to Apple menu > System Settings > General, then clicking on Software Update. Bugs or driver compatibility issues could hypothetically be interfering. Some accessories may also rely on proprietary software to do much of anything, in which case you'll want to make sure the latest version of that is installed. Check the Mac App Store or a manufacturer's website. External cards and drives need to use file system formats compatible with macOS, namely APFS, FAT or ExFAT.

Lastly, be sure that you're using the right type of USB cable for a device's power and bandwidth requirements and that there's no damage or debris interfering with either cables or ports. Things like lint and bent pins may be all it takes to block something from working. Lint, dust and grime can typically be cleaned out with an air can or by gently swiping a soft-tipped tool. As for damage, you'll likely just need to swap in a new cable, especially if there are any signs of browning or exposed wires. Those signal a fire hazard.

Colchester United vs Ipswich Town U21 - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

By Sam Varley | 09 Nov 2026 00:55

Colchester United and Ipswich Town Under-21s will square off on Tuesday in the final round of group-stage games in the EFL Trophy.

Both teams are winless in the competition and unable to advance from the group, leaving them only in search of a confidence-boosting victory.

Colchester United return to action on Tuesday aiming to make it back-to-back cup victories having ended a winless streak at the weekend.

They have endured a tough start to 2026-27, currently sitting 23rd in League Two having earned just 12 points from their first 13 matches while only winning two and losing five of those.

Their last league victory came in late September, and a 12-game winless run also included their first two EFL Trophy outings, losing 3-1 away at Peterborough United and drawing 1-1 against Luton Town before losing the resultant penalty shootout.

That rut then culminated in consecutive league defeats to Northampton Town and Walsall, sending Danny Cowley's side into the relegation zone, before they hosted Worcester City in the FA Cup first round of Saturday and finally bounced back to winning ways as Jaden Williams scored the only goal.

Now unable to advance in the EFL Trophy, but needing to build confidence on the back of that win before turning their focus back to their League Two survival bid, Colchester United will aim to make it consecutive triumphs at the Colchester Community Stadium.

Their visitors, similarly, are unable to go further but will look to record a first win of this season's tournament to go out on a positive note.

Ipswich Town Under-21s began the 2026-27 EFL Trophy with group-stage trips to Luton Town and Peterborough United and struggled to compete on both occasions, firstly losing 6-0 to the latter in September.

John McGreal's side then suffered a 4-1 beating against Peterborough in the second round of games, having fallen 4-0 down before the break before Luca Fletcher netted a second-half consolation.

They have since enjoyed more success in the National League Cup, most recently bouncing back to winning ways with a 5-0 triumph away at Aldershot last week with Frankie Runham hitting a brace alongside goals from Jakubas Mazionis, Josh Lewis and Shakil Nicolaou.

Ipswich Town Under-21s now head to Colchester aiming to put their first two EFL Trophy results behind them and bow out with a first win over league opposition in the competition.

Ipswich Town U21 form (all competitions):

Teddy Bishop, Harry Anderson, Kylian Kouassi and Jake Leake will remain sidelined by injuries.

Williams will continue to lead the line after his winning goal against Worcester City, while Jack Payne should continue in support in an experienced midfield unit alongside Jay Williams and Paul Digby.

Ipswich Town Under-21s should field a similar starting XI from their 5-0 win last time out, with regular Luca Fletcher likely to continue up front.

Runham is a danger man on the wing having moved onto six goals for the season with his brace, while Nicolaou has a claim to come in from the start after scoring off the bench.

Mazionis and Charlie Compton are mainstays in defence and midfield respectively.

The young Tractor Boys have their best chance to compete in an EFL Trophy game on Tuesday, and we do see them keeping it close but back Cowley's men to make it-back-to-back wins if he sticks with a full-strength lineup.

Chevy Blazer EV Police Cars Have Almost 500 HP And Are Used Performance Bargains

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

True, most police cars don't come with V8 engines anymore, but that doesn't mean the cops have given up chasing people — which is unfortunate, since high-speed chases are dangerous for everyone. The difference is just that current police interceptors are now available with alternative forms of motivation. The 498-horsepower Chevrolet Blazer EV Police Pursuit Vehicle is a case in point: The second-quickest PPV in this year's Michigan State Police testing (PDF) – behind only the Mustang Mach-E GT eAWD — the Blazer is propelled by a pair of electric motors. And now that Chevy has been selling them to police departments for a couple of years, some are starting to come up on the used market. They're practically a steal, too, with examples listed below $30k on sites such as CarGurus, so you're getting a lot of horses for not a lot of money.

Among new cars, access to 500 horsepower will cost you at least about $25,000 more than that, regardless of powerplant. A gas-powered Dodge Charger Scat Pack, for instance, serves up 550 hp with an MSRP of $56,990 with destination. A Tesla Model 3 Performance, with 528 ponies, is listed at $56,630. Looking at the numbers based on price, the few new vehicles available under $30,000, such as the base model Honda Accord and Nissan Leaf, check in with 192 and 214 hp, respectively. One thing to keep in mind, though, is that the supply of used Chevy Blazer EV police cars is relatively limited. Only five were available nationwide on Carfax when this article was written, with prices ranging from $29,405 to $35,995.

The key distinction between the two comes down to the tuning of their powertrains. The EV PPV is based on the high-performance Blazer SS sold for retail customers. That's a machine we called a normal family crossover with supercar speed, thanks to its output of 615 hp and 650 lb-ft of torque. Of course, with great power comes great responsibility, which may be why the cops can't tap into all that output. Specific police tuning limits the Blazer EV PPV to 498 horsepower and 571 lb-ft. Still, the PPV is no slouch when it comes to acceleration and speed. Per testing with the Michigan State Police, it can reach 60 mph in a mere 5.14 seconds with a top speed of 130 mph.

And while that's a fairly large spread between the pre-owned police Blazers and the civilian range-toppers, the latter is also roughly twice the price. Chevrolet wants $62,495 for a 2027 Blazer SS EV with all-wheel drive after destination. The least expensive brand-new Blazer EV is $46,795 after destination.

Another important difference between the cop car and regular Blazer is that the former has a purpose-built electrical architecture with a 400- to 12-volt power module reducer and a universal vehicle module. These components help the Blazer PPV provide enough juice to power upfitter equipment and allow it to handle unique operation modes such as Shutdown Inhibit or Fast Idle. Also in the mix are front Brembo brakes, a heavy-duty suspension, and steel skid plates. And while some used examples still have bull bars attached, the red and blue flashing lights are not.

Which Canon Cameras Are Compatible With Its Free 3D Photo Software?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Though not every camera is compatible there are a number on the market that are a perfect fit.

Canon wants to add a third dimension to your photos, and you won't need those pesky glasses to use it. At least, that's the goal of its Dual Pixel 3D imaging software. Released in October 2026, the free software's 3D converter app allows users to generate immersive 3D images and models without the use of specialized equipment.

Before you jump into 3D model rendering, you should know not every camera is compatible with Canon's software tool. This is because the system relies on your camera's Dual Pixel CMOS AF, a sensor-based autofocus found on select iterations of the company's EOS R series, which includes several of Engadget's highest-rated cameras of 2026.

Introduced by the camera maker in 2013, the sensor gives each pixel two photodiodes, which can either be combined to form a 2D image or read independently. Dual Pixel 3D runs these phase differences through a proprietary algorithm that converts your still image into a 3D rendering. Exportable in OBJ, GLB, and MP4 video formats, the images add hyper-realistic depth and texture to your photos.

Notably, the software is currently only compatible with the 2024 version or later of Windows 11. Once downloaded, extract the ZIP file and open the installer. After navigating through location, language, and licensing agreement windows, install the app and kick off your 3D journey. For a look at the Dual Pixel 3D Converter's instruction manual, visit Canon's website.

Before diving into how to use Canon's 3D imaging software, let's make sure you are using a compatible camera and lens. You can find a full list of cameras and lenses below or on Canon's website.

Canon's 3D imaging software requires a select group of EOS R cameras. The EOS R is Canon's most advanced series, featuring the company's lauded RF mount. With a 54mm diameter, the mount enables photographers to use more advanced lenses, decreasing the distance between the lens and senser. The mirrorless camera possesses a high FPS shutter, 12-pin communication system, cinematic 4k video at 120p, 8-stop image stabilization, and enhanced subject detection make the camera series Canon's most powerful — and expensive. The Canon EOS R5 Mark II, in particular, has garnered rave reviews from our writers.

There is, however, a catch. The earliest EOS R-series camera compatible with Canon's 3D modeling system is the EOS R5, which was released in 2020.  Compatible cameras include:

You will also need to pair your EOS R-series camera with a compatible RF lens. The full list of compatible lenses include:

You can take several steps to maximize the quality of your 3D image. First, set your camera's aperture settings between f/5.6 and f/8. This keeps your subject in depth and reduces blurring and conversion errors. To boot, shoot at ISO 1600 or lower to minimize the noise in your photograph. Note that 3D models cannot be generated at ISO's greater than 3200. In your camera settings, you should also enable your Dual Pixel RAW or Add Distance to JPEGs settings, depending on whether you are taking RAW or JPEG images, respectively.

In terms of subject matter, the camera manufacturer suggests photographing objects with matte textures and thick, simple shapes. Examples include flowers with rounded petals, plants and leaves with little gloss, ceramic items, shallow plates, and food with rounded shapes. Moreover, avoid subjects with slender shapes, strong reflections, multiple blind spots, or significant blur.

When taking the photograph, center the subject and leave plenty of surrounding space. Shooting from a slightly elevated angle will help reduce blind spots and keep the subject at a distance 5 – 10x the focal length away. If the subject is dark, use the camera's flash or external lighting.

After you've taken your photo, import your image into the Dual Pixel 3D Converter software. Once booted up, you will find the thumbnail of your most recent photographs in the browser located on the lefthand side of the screen. From here, you can use the 3D Model Settings tab to customize your 3D generation process. Once generated, you can export your OBJ, GLB, or MP4 file to external 3D editing software. From there, the world is your 3D-oyster.

Researchers tested what happens to student grades when schools completely ban cell phones in classrooms

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Students who dropped their phones in a box before every lecture earned higher grades, and the phone ban helped struggling students most.

Schools around the world are taking phones away at the classroom door, mostly in the belief that such a ban has to help. Almost no large randomized experiment had tested whether it does. Now one has, and the phones went into wooden boxes.

College students in eastern India who dropped their phones in a box at the start of every lecture finished the semester with slightly higher grades than classmates who kept theirs. Attendance didn’t change, so the researchers read the gain as better attention in class rather than more time in it.

Alp Sungu of the Wharton School at the University of Pennsylvania led the study. His co-authors were Pradeep Kumar Choudhury of Jawaharlal Nehru University in New Delhi and Andreas Bjerre-Nielsen of the University of Copenhagen.

The trial covered 16,683 students, 742 teachers, and 2,305 courses.

In an interview with Earth.com, Sungu said the gains were largest for lower-performing students, and he was careful about what that means for schools elsewhere.

“That doesn’t guarantee the same ban will work everywhere,” he said. “Banning phones is not a silver bullet, but it is plausibly a simple, low-cost policy lever worth considering.”

The experiment ran at 10 colleges and universities in Odisha, an eastern Indian state of more than 40 million people. There, more than 70 percent of people aged 15 to 29 own a smartphone.

The team had about 1,000 wooden deposit boxes built from recycled materials and put one in every classroom. A full-time research assistant at each campus kept the protocol running.

Departments were split by year of study into 420 groups, and a random draw assigned 198 of them to the ban. In those groups, every student handed over their phone before each lecture for the whole spring semester of 2024, from February to May. The policy wasn’t optional.

Students with a documented emergency or caregiving duty could tell the teacher beforehand, and everyone else went without. Students in the other groups kept their usual access.

The boxes stood in their classrooms too, so the only difference between the two arms was whether a teacher enforced the rule.

The participants averaged 19 years old, ranged from 16 to 22, and about a third were women. Grades came from end-of-semester exams that outside examiners wrote and marked, with no devices allowed for either group.

Across everyone in the trial, the ban raised grades by a small amount. It came to about a third of the difference that a very good teacher, rather than an average one, makes over a school year.

That puts it in the same range as large curriculum reforms and teacher training programs, which the authors use as the benchmark.

The average hides where the change happened. Students whose earlier grades fell below the middle of the pack improved, and so did first-year undergraduates.

Students who were already doing well, postgraduates, and students in science, technology, engineering, and math fields showed no measurable change.

Because the weakest students moved most, the spread of grades grew less in the banned classrooms than in the others. Attendance, measured from the colleges’ own records, didn’t shift in either direction.

The grades themselves are hard to inflate.

Under Odisha’s rules, 60 to 80 percent of each course mark comes from external exams graded off-site by people who don’t know the student. The ban’s effect was largest in non-STEM courses, where that outside share is 80 percent.

So teachers going easy on their own students is an unlikely explanation, though the authors couldn’t rule it out entirely.

A subset of 2,557 students answered two surveys over the semester. Students who had spent the term under the ban became more supportive of phone restrictions than those who hadn’t.

They also rated the restrictions as more beneficial and were less likely to want a classroom with no rules at all.

Given five options, from an all-day ban to no ban, the banned students favored stricter policies than the control group did, with the biggest shift toward an all-day ban. The trial only ever tested a lecture-only ban.

Asked by Earth.com what surprised him about how students felt, Sungu pointed to that shift in support. “That’s encouraging for implementation, since policies students support are easier to sustain,” he said.

The surveys found no change in how distracted students felt, how much they thought they were learning, their well-being, or their motivation. Students under the ban reported a mild rise in the fear of missing out, and no change in how much time they spent on their phones.

Women in the banned groups backed the policy more strongly, while men reported a small rise in missing-out feelings and in screen time.

Research assistants also made random spot checks, glancing into each classroom through a window or doorway and ticking off a list of behaviors without interrupting the lecture.

In banned classrooms, they recorded less disruptive behavior and less chatter unrelated to the lesson, and teachers who were busier keeping order and more engaged with their material.

The checks also caught something the team hadn’t planned to measure. “To our surprise, our random classroom spot checks showed that teachers also used their phones in class, and the ban reduced their phone use as well,” Sungu told Earth.com. Teachers were never subject to the rule themselves.

One reading came out the other way. To the observers, students in banned classrooms looked more distracted than students who had their phones.

The authors suggested that with the phone gone, a student’s attention goes to whatever is next most noticeable, such as background noise. That kind of restlessness is easier for an observer to spot.

The students in this trial were in college, having already passed entrance hurdles, and the policy fight in most countries is about younger children.

Since first-year students and weaker students gained most, the authors think the effect could be larger at earlier ages, but that remains to be tested. The study itself ran for one semester in one Indian state.

The ban covered lectures only. Whether a bell-to-bell ban, phones locked away through breaks as well, changes how students treat each other or use their phones at home is an open question.

So is whether the result holds in richer countries, where students often have other devices alongside the phone.

Sungu’s next question is about a different technology. “There have been recent proposals to ban AI in classrooms, but such bans could be a double-edged sword, and we need more scientific evidence to guide policy,” he said.

He also named delaying young people’s exposure to social media and AI companions as an area worth studying.

Until a trial like this one runs in a middle school, the evidence for phone bans rests on a semester of wooden boxes in Odisha. It also rests on students who ended that semester more in favor of the rule than their classmates who kept their phones.

The full study was published in the journal Science.

Captain America: The First Avenger's Director Was Supposed To Tackle A Much Bigger Marvel Hero

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

In retrospect, Joe Johnston is an extremely important director in the history of the Marvel Cinematic Universe. After all, it was his "Captain America: The First Avenger" that found the MCU's emotional core and arguably its greatest hero upon its release in 2011, all while teeing up "The Avengers." The movie also served as our introduction to Chris Evans as Steve Rogers, who became a real anchor for this franchise. But before Johnston put his stamp on Cap, he nearly tackled a very different, and far bigger, Marvel superhero.

Prior to the MCU's success, Marvel sold off most of its character rights to various Hollywood studios. That's why Sony owns the movie rights to "Spider-Man" and the same reason why Universal Pictures holds the rights to the Incredible Hulk and solo films based on that character. In fact, the studio spent years developing a movie about the big green guy in the lead up to Ang Lee ("Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon") bringing "Hulk" to a theater near you in 2003.

Johnston, as it were, was among the filmmakers once set to helm a movie based on that very Marvel Comics character (as created by Stan Lee and Jack Kirby). In a 1997 Variety report breaking down the "mania for Marvel" that was going on in Hollywood at the time, it was revealed that Johnston, coming off "Jumanji," was working on an "Incredible Hulk" feature film with screenwriter Jonathan Hensleigh ("Armageddon").

This was even before "Blade" became both a modest box office hit and one of the most important movies ever made, as it proved Marvel could deliver a mainstream success (and not just direct-to-video fare). Once "X-Men" hit in 2000, there was no longer any doubt. But back when Johnston was attached to his Hulk project, that wasn't the case.

As Variety reported in 1998, Jonathan Hensleigh was set to make his feature directorial debut on a Hulk movie before it was placed on the back burner. (By that point, the studio had already spent $20 million on development.) As for Joe Johnston, it's unclear why he departed the film, but he went on to direct "October Sky" and "Jurassic Park III" instead.

Even though "Hulk" was based on a very popular character, Ang Lee's movie was by no means a success. Against a budget of $137 million, it made just $245 million at the box office. Universal moved quickly after Sam Raimi's "Spider-Man" saved superhero movies from being just a fad, delivering record-breaking levels of success. It was the first film to ever make at least $100 million on a single weekend. That opened the floodgates.

Lee's "Hulk" didn't pan out commercially, but it's undeniable that Hulk himself was one of Marvel's most popular characters at that time. Captain America, as hard as it may be to fathom now, wasn't on the same level back then. It was in no small part thanks to Joe Johnston's work on "The First Avenger" that the tide changed. One year later, "The Avengers" became a box office hit that redefined blockbuster filmmaking. If Johnston's solo Cap movie hadn't worked, who knows if that big crossover would have panned out as well.

By MCU standards, "The First Avenger" was a modest success, pulling in $370 million at the box office. However, it's come to be regarded as one of the MCU's most underrated movies to date. That's largely thanks to Joe Johnston.

You can grab "Captain America: The First Avenger" on 4K, Blu-ray, or DVD from Amazon.

The disadvantages of buying tech from other countries

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Importing tech isn't too hard, but the downsides may not be worth it.

There are many reasons to import phones or other tech, whether you're buying it in person during a trip or via online marketplaces or importers. Your phone could die while you're travelling abroad, for example, forcing you to buy a replacement. Or maybe there's a perfect product for you, but it's not available where you live. Either way, buying tech from other countries is sometimes the only option.

Depending on the product, though, this can come with a catch or two. Buying devices from other regions can come with annoying restrictions. You might end up with slightly different hardware, lack warranty coverage or, in the worst case, not have access to core software or connectivity features. Here's what you should know before buying a new phone, laptop or any other tech product from overseas.

A device can have different hardware configurations globally. In China, for example, certain iPhone models have slots for two physical SIMs. Conversely, US iPhones dropped the SIM tray starting with the iPhone 14, so you must use an eSIM instead. iPhones sold in China also get limited access to Apple Intelligence tools, even if you use it elsewhere.

Phones may pose issues with cellular bands. Manufacturers usually offer different versions of the same phone designed for specific bands in a given region. If you buy a phone in another country, it might not work on the US' 5G network, for example. This means you could lose access to carrier-specific features like VoLTE or Wi-Fi Calling if the device you're buying isn't certified for your network. It may seem minor, but it can be a headache if you plan to use it as your main device.

Other tech isn't free from potential issues, either. Laptops, for instance, can have different keyboard layouts depending on where you buy them. French laptops will use the AZERTY layout, and not every brand offers a QWERTY version in such markets. Depending on the product, power can be an issue too: imported products will likely use a different plug, so you'll need an adaptor, but that's not all. Watch out for voltages as well; the US uses 120V at 60Hz, but most of Europe and Asia use 220V to 240V, usually at 50Hz. Phone and laptop chargers usually work worldwide, but not all tech will.

Warranty is also an issue depending on what you're buying. Many manufacturers only provide tech support in the region where the device was sold. And if you're buying a product that isn't officially sold in the US, getting a broken part repaired can be tough, too. Some companies are more flexible when it comes to supporting devices purchased abroad, as is the case with AppleCare. Still, the company's warranty terms state that it may limit service for select devices, including the iPhone, to the country where the product was originally sold. Buying locally is still a safer bet if warranty is a priority.

If you're buying online, you also have to contend with import duties and shipping costs. The US has eliminated its $800 de minimis exemption for international parcels, so even small packages may get taxed quite heavily. International shipping isn't cheap, either. Most of the time, then, you have to really decide whether what you want to buy overseas is worth it.

Paris Saint-Germain Women vs Bayern Munich Women - prediction, team news, lineups

Like PSG, Bayern are one of eight teams yet to lose in the 2026-27 league phase after collecting five points from their first three games.

The reigning Frauen-Bundesliga champions kicked off the main draw with a 2-2 draw against Manchester City before they eased to a 3-0 victory over Benfica in Portugal.

Jose Barcala's side then played out a second straight 2-2 home in Europe, fighting back from two goals down to salvage a point against Real Madrid.

They have since returned to winning ways with victories over Bayer Leverkusen and Werder Bremen in the DFB-Pokal Frauen and Frauen-Bundesliga respectively, extending their unbeaten start to the season to 15 games (W13, D2).

In fact, Bayern have not lost a competitive game since falling to a 4-2 defeat against Barcelona in the second leg of their UWCL semi-final tie in May.

They will be hopeful about their chances of extending that unbeaten streak with a win, having prevailed in their last two away games against PSG, including a 3-1 success in last season's league phase.

Just a quarter of U.S. adults trust creators to always disclose brand deals

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Just one in four U.S. adults believe content creators consistently disclose brand ties when promoting a sponsored product.

That’s not a lot of trust for an industry that relies on creators building close bonds with their audiences.

The data comes from a Morning Consult survey conducted for Business Insider; around 2,000 people participated.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

Perhaps unsurprisingly, older folks were less inclined to trust creators: 53% of Baby Boomers said creators “rarely or never” clearly disclose paid deals, while just 21% of Gen Z said the same thing.

Morning Consult also asked respondents whether creators should have to disclose paid sponsorships at all. Across all age groups, the majority of people said yes.

But some did say no: 12% of Boomers, 18% of Gen Xers, 22% of Millennials, and 23% of Gen Z’ers.

We find this wild, and if you’ll forgive a second of conjecture, we’re wondering if the striking number of nos among Millennials and Gen Z has to do with people in those generations being raised in a culture of online side hustles and low barriers to content creation. How many of these respondents are creators themselves, or wish they were, or think they could be, if they just started making TikTok Shop promo videos like their friends and coworkers?

To them, it might seem like a drag to make a video hoping to earn a quick buck, only to have to admit “Yeah, I’m selling something,” in an obvious place.

It seems that way to some established creators and brands, too. BI also cited a recent survey from SheSpeaks where 14% of creators admitted they don’t always disclose brand ties, and 18% said that within the past year, a brand had explicitly asked them not to disclose that they had been paid.

If you’re a regular Tubefilter reader, you will of course know that behavior is very against Federal Trade Commission regulations, and could open violative creators and businesses up to hefty fines. There is never an excuse for an individual creator failing to disclose, but a business that makes it standard to push creators not to disclose is even more problematic.

And while disclosures might seem like a drag to these creators and businesses, to most other people, transparency is reassuring.

Emily Steele, CEO of creator marketing platform Hummingbirds, told BI another key aspect here is that creators need to have meaningful and engaging content outside of brand deals for their audiences to be open when they endorse a product.

“There are creators who only post UGC or sponsored content without any cohesion around what they’re posting about and why,” she said. “Why would I, as a follower, trust you when I feel like all you post is a paid brand deal?”
